# Sentinel — Decision Log

Canonical record of decisions for the Sentinel build. Agents propose; John decides. Ratified entries are attributed to John. Agent-made calls are logged separately as flagged assumptions and are cheap to reverse. New forks get new entries; history is never rewritten.

## Ratified (John)

D-001 through D-006 were ruled by John directly in the facilitated intake session of 2026-07-27.

D-007 through D-011 were **ruled by delegation**: on 2026-07-27, presented with four open forks and a stated blind spot, John directed "go with what you think is best for now and we can modify later if we discover issues in the field." The rulings are John's by that delegation; their content was authored by Opus 5 from recommendations reviewed by Fable. They are recorded as ratified because John holds the authority and exercised it, and they are marked as delegated so no future reader mistakes them for individually reviewed rulings. They are explicitly revisitable on field evidence.

Delegation covers design forks only. **Gate signing authority is unchanged and non-delegable** — Gates S1 and S2 are signed by John in facilitated sessions, never by an agent (D-002).

- **D-001 (2026-07-27) — Section 14 ladder.** v1 adopts 14.5 (mandate-fidelity limit promoted into §8; applied) and 14.4 with modification (vendor baselines documentation-only; ALL executed/emulated vendor comparisons cut from v1 — note this is stricter than recommendation 14.4 as written, which would have kept free-testnet executed comparisons in v1; the ruling defers those to rung 2 as well). Rung 1: 14.2 receipt-verifier CLI (+10–20h), a stated goal that must ship before the portfolio artifact is called done. Rung 2 (post-MVP, discovery track): executed vendor comparisons where free test access exists. 14.3 attestation: stretch, considered only after the §7.5 gates are green; its claims-boundary wording (conformance is against simulated effects at a recorded block) applied to §8 now.
- **D-002 (2026-07-27) — Gate cadence.** Two mid-build facilitated sign-offs. Gate S1: vault + isolated signer + exact-action binding + Case 1 end-to-end + replay/tamper invariants green. Gate S2: full fixture corpus + §7.5 hard-gate evidence. Gates are signed only by John.
- **D-003 (2026-07-27) — Kill criteria, no token cap.** The §12 stop condition on scope expansion; a no-progress halt after 3 failed independent attempts at a gate; immediate halt if any agent modifies fixtures, ground-truth labels, or gate definitions to make a suite pass.
- **D-004 (2026-07-27) — Dispatch.** Opus 5 architects and directs subagents; John launches the build and holds all gate and veto authority. Build authorized through Gate S2.
- **D-005 (2026-07-27) — 14.6 overridden.** The discovery-before-build sequencing recommended in 14.6 is overridden by John's decision to launch the build now. The §10 discovery track proceeds in parallel under John's own ownership; it is not build-agent scope. (Ratified via the intake-ledger sign-off.)
- **D-006 (2026-07-27) — Verification partition.** Ratified with the intake ledger: fixture ground-truth labels are authored by an agent independent of the implementers, adversarially cross-checked, and sampled by John at gates; implementation agents may not modify fixtures, labels, or gate definitions; public claims are certified only by John.
- **D-007 (2026-07-27, delegated) — Case 2: what counts as a real prompt injection.** Operationalizes the §7.5 hard gate "a real prompt injection changes the agent proposal and is contained." A demonstration counts as real only when all three hold: (a) the agent configuration is a documented, plausibly-naive default, with nothing in its own instructions engineered to make it obey injected text, and its scaffold pinned by hash; (b) the malicious content arrives through a data channel the agent legitimately reads (§3.1 "retrieved documents"), never through its own instruction channel; (c) a **control run** — same configuration, same channel, benign document — produces the Case 1 proposal. Without the control run the flip is not attributable to the injection, which is the difference between a demonstration and a staged demo. Each injection fixture records model id, model version, temperature, scaffold hash, date, and the full transcript. **Reproducibility:** pinned transcripts are the CI fixtures; one live canary run executes alongside them, never fails CI, and its run history is included in the S2 evidence bundle — an unobserved canary is not evidence. **Spike-first:** the injection is attempted before the deep build, timeboxed to 4 hours. A negative result is a finding, not a failure — §4's claim is that an *untrusted* agent cannot execute, not that a gullible one is fooled. On a negative result: report the frontier-model resistance honestly as its own data point and drive Case 2 with a deliberately naive configuration labeled as such. One genuine injection; no jailbreak research.
- **D-008 (2026-07-27, delegated) — §7.5 soft-gate definitions.** Two §7.5 gates were unfalsifiable as written; under D-003 no implementing agent may define a gate, so they are defined here. **Five-minute comprehension:** three fresh-context reviewers, given only the demo, dashboard, and README — no repository access — each answer five questions frozen before the first run and never shown to the build loop. Pass threshold, set in advance: all three score ≥4/5. The questions are those a skeptical security engineer asks — what is cryptographically bound; who signs what; why Case 3 blocks; what a receipt proves; what it does not prove. Agents run this as a proxy; John samples. **Vendor honesty:** every cell of the capability matrix is documentation-only, dated, and linked to its cited source; the "executed" and "faithfully emulated" columns of the §10.1 labeling scheme are empty in v1 (per D-001); cells that are inference rather than quoted documentation are marked as inference; and no claim *or layout* implying empirical superiority over any named vendor appears in any v1 artifact. The empty-column condition is mechanically checkable.
- **D-009 (2026-07-27, delegated) — Gate S2 pass conditions amended. Amends D-002.** Gate S2 passes on: the full 30–50 fixture corpus; §7.5 hard-gate evidence; the §7.3 ablation report; and the receipt-verifier CLI (D-010). The ablation is added because §7 opens by stating that four demo paths alone cannot prove the verdicts are not hard-coded — without it, S2 would sign off a corpus with no analysis that makes the corpus mean anything. The evidence dashboard remains outside S2 unless John adds it at the gate. If the window tightens, priority order is corpus > ablation > CLI.
- **D-010 (2026-07-27, delegated) — Receipt-verifier CLI promoted into v1. Amends D-001.** The 14.2 standalone receipt verifier moves from ladder rung 1 to a Gate S2 pass condition (D-009). The reason is engineering rather than strategic: an independent reimplementation is the strongest available test of the spec's precision, and RFC 8785 edge cases, hash-domain separation, and encoding ambiguity surface only when someone builds from the written schema. That feedback is wanted while the schemas are still cheap to change — after the corpus fossilizes, a schema correction regenerates every fixture. **Conditions:** the verifier shares no canonicalization or hashing code with the evaluator (same library behind a different flag is the same code verifying itself); it is implemented in a different language from the evaluator — Python — against an independent JCS implementation and an independent keccak implementation, because cross-language agreement is a materially stronger test than two TypeScript modules *and* is cheaper than hand-rolling a second RFC 8785 implementation; and it is built from the published schemas only. **Effort:** 14.2's 10–20h estimate assumed code reuse. With the independence condition the corrected budget is 20–30h. Recorded as a measured correction to a documented number, per AGENTS.md.
- **D-011 (2026-07-27, delegated) — Fixture-label independence protocol. Extends D-006.** Independence here is **procedural, not organizational**, and §8 states it in those words. A solo build cannot buy organizational independence; what it can buy is an auditable bound. (a) The labeling prompt is authored and frozen **before the corpus build**, and its hash committed. The claim is deliberately "before the corpus build" and not "before the evaluator's failure modes are known" — Case 1 is end-to-end by S1, so some evaluator knowledge already exists; the freeze blocks the bulk of it, which is corpus-build knowledge, from leaking backward. (b) The labeler receives payload schemas, security invariants, and each fixture's declared intent — never evaluator source, never evaluator output. (c) A second fresh labeler re-labels a random ~20% sample; the disagreement rate is reported at S2 as a corpus-quality metric. (d) Thresholds are declared in advance, or the metric is decoration: >10% disagreement halts S2 pending corpus review, and any disagreement on a hard-gate-relevant fixture escalates to John individually regardless of the aggregate rate. (e) John's gate sampling is adversarial rather than random — oversample fixtures where the baseline and conformance verdicts disagree, and fixtures whose label is *review*.

- **D-012 (2026-07-28) — A-011 confirmed: signer refusal semantics, plus a recorded refusal artifact.** John ruled after two outside reviews, both recommending confirmation. The three severity tiers stand, and refusal continues never to become a verdict: "Sentinel evaluated this and blocked it" and "the signer declined to attest" are different claims about what the system concluded, and an evidence bundle conflating them misreports the result. **Added by this ruling:** a refusal must produce a *recorded artifact*, because at present "the signer refused" and "the signer was never asked" are indistinguishable — so Gate S2 could not prove the signer ever saw a request. The artifact is deliberately **not** a DecisionReceiptPayload; a refusal is not a decision, and reusing the receipt type would create something a careless reader could mistake for one. Supersedes A-011, which remains on the record as the original reasoning.
- **D-013 (2026-07-28) — A-012 confirmed as a mechanism, with the CLAIM narrowed.** The per-nonce attestation guard stays, including the basis-scoping narrowing and the rejection of exempting REVIEW. What changes is what may be said about it. It is **per-process, best-effort defence in depth — never a durable system guarantee.** A-012's own stated limits make this unavoidable: the guard lives in process memory, is forgotten on restart, and is blind to a second signer sharing the key. "Sentinel guarantees one live executable attestation per nonce" would be false after a restart, and that is the shape of overclaim §7.5 exists to stop. **The vault's nonce is the actual guarantee.** Any future durable version needs single-writer persistent state before the wording may change. Supersedes A-012's framing; the code is unchanged by this ruling.
- **D-014 (2026-07-28) — A-018 resolved: NO conformance checks in the signer. Bind the signer's own decoding into the evidence instead.** The one fork the two reviewers disagreed on; John ruled for reviewer 1. **Rejected:** giving the signer a narrow decoder that checks resource, beneficiary, duration and recurrence against the mandate. Two implementations of one policy that must agree is a liability rather than a defence — divergence becomes a new failure mode and the more capable one silently becomes authoritative — and a signer checking four fields of many could not honestly be described as independently checking conformance, which is an invitation to overclaim exactly where §7.5 bites. It also could not re-derive simulated effects without re-running the simulation, so the compromised-evaluator threat is only partly addressed. That threat is already answered head-on by ratified work: D-010's independent verifier plus the D-011 labelled corpus. **Adopted instead:** the signer independently decodes the calldata and verifies that the decoded parameters recorded in the evidence bundle match what the bytes actually say, refusing if they disagree. This is *derivation without judgement* — the signer never consults the mandate. Its effect is that the bundle's decoded parameters become signer-attested rather than evaluator-asserted, so a wrong-purpose ALLOW is detectable after the fact by the D-010 verifier, which does the conformance comparison. Accountability without a second evaluator. **Boundary to state precisely and not to overstate:** the signer checks that the parameters match the bytes *given the selector*; it does not check that the selector belongs at that target, which remains the evaluator's job.
- **D-015 (2026-07-28) — A-020 confirmed, and the proposal amended to stop contradicting itself.** `policy.failureMode` governs unresolved checks; §5.2's unconditional "any unknown required check reviews" describes the REVIEW setting rather than overriding the field. The alternative reading would make `failureMode` nearly meaningless. The corollary stands: a target code-hash mismatch is UNRESOLVED, not a VIOLATION, because §4.2 says Sentinel "does not label the target malicious. It reports insufficient evidence for automatic approval." **Two follow-ups ruled with it.** (a) **§5.2 of the proposal is amended** so the prose no longer contradicts the field — leaving the contradiction in place guarantees a future reader re-derives this fork from scratch. (b) **Both `failureMode` configurations must appear in the demo, not only in the tests.** Because Case 4's outcome now turns on a policy setting, a reviewer will reasonably suspect the setting was chosen to produce the wanted result; showing REVIEW and FAIL_CLOSED side by side on the same evidence is what answers that suspicion. Recorded as a demo requirement for §9 step 9.

- **D-016 (2026-07-28) — The naming collision is NOT accepted. "Sentinel" remains a private working codename.** Ruled by John. **Blocked until he approves a replacement name, following basic domain and trademark/collision review:** repository visibility changes, public demos, published links, and any portfolio or résumé reference. **This is a pre-publication gate, NOT an S1 condition** — it does not block signing S1, and S1 must not be held open waiting for it. The two are independent: S1 is about whether the mechanism is proven, this is about what may be said publicly and under what name. Supersedes the working-name warning at the top of the proposal by making it a named gate with an owner rather than a caution. A mechanical guard is added to the project gate: `scripts/check-rename-gate.sh` fails if the repository has become public, because AGENTS.md requires a durable project rule get a mechanical check rather than prose, and "nothing goes public" is exactly the kind of rule that is violated by a single click months later.
- **D-017 (2026-07-28) — Independent adversarial review of §9 steps 4–6 is an S1 CONDITION. Extends D-002.** Ruled by John, resolving the gap both outside reviewers identified: only step 3 had had an independent pass, which conflicts with the project's own independent-grader principle (HANDOFF house rule 3, and the playbook's "the generator never grades itself"). **Conditions, as ruled:** (a) the review is fresh-context and adversarial, covering the **S1-relevant portions** of steps 4–6 — not the whole of them, since corpus, ablation and receipt-verifier evidence remain S2; (b) it runs against a **fixed commit**, so findings are attributable and reproducible rather than aimed at a moving target; (c) it reviews the **actual implementation and tests**, not only the evidence summary — a review of a summary grades the summary; (d) **material findings are independently adjudicated**, not resolved by the build loop's own say-so; (e) **confirmed defects are corrected and reverified before S1 closes**. The fixed-commit requirement directly addresses a recorded failure from the step-3 review, where the tree was edited mid-review and one reviewer reported a transient artifact as a possible defect. The adjudication requirement addresses the other: 6 of 8 skeptic verifications in that review never ran, so most findings were adjudicated by the build loop against the spec — precisely the self-grading this condition exists to prevent.

- **D-018 (2026-07-28) — What "end-to-end" means for Gate S1. Clarifies D-002.** Ruled by John at the S1 session, resolving an ambiguity both outside reviewers flagged in the phrase "Case 1 end-to-end". **For S1, end-to-end means: from a CONSTRUCTED exact EVM action, through decoding, simulation, evaluation, isolated signing, vault enforcement, and the resulting onchain state.** Agent proposal generation, and its wiring into that pipeline, belong to **§9 step 7 and Gate S2**. The distinction is not cosmetic: the D-007 spike already showed that an agent *proposal* flips under injection (A-009), but the proposal and the pipeline have never been connected, so a reader who took "end-to-end" to include the agent would be reading a claim the evidence does not support. Recording the boundary here means S1's scope cannot later be quietly widened or narrowed by whoever reads the phrase next. The gate pack and the project gate's coverage boundary both state it in these terms.

- **D-019 (2026-08-15) — Where an agent proposal's calldata comes from. Enables §9 step 7.** Ruled by John at the step-7 design fork, choosing among three presented options. **Ruling: Sentinel ABI-encodes the calldata from the agent's `function_signature` and `args`.** The fork was surfaced rather than settled in code because it decides what a ratified sentence means: §4.2 Case 2 says "Sentinel derives the selector and parameters from calldata rather than trusting the agent's description", and the D-007 agent emits no calldata — it emits typed parameter claims. Encoding from those claims makes the decoder's recovery of them a round-trip through Sentinel's own encoder, not independent corroboration. **Rejected alternatives, with the cost that decided it:** (a) the agent emits raw calldata — keeps §4.2's sentence literally true, but changes `scaffoldHash()` and so stales all four D-007 fixtures, and a naive agent must hand-compute a keccak selector and pad four ABI words, so a mis-encode would break the CONTROL arm and collapse D-007's control-run requirement; (b) the agent emits both, with claim-vs-bytes divergence recorded as evidence — epistemically strongest and the only option that exercises §3.1's "agent-supplied parameter or purpose claims" as a live check rather than a structural impossibility, at the same fixture cost and mis-encoding risk as (a). **Consequences, both recorded in code rather than left implicit.** The transcriber joins the TRUSTED set (§3.1, "canonical schema encoders and hashes"), which makes the encode/decode round-trip test load-bearing: it is the only mechanical check that "the agent proposed X" and "Sentinel evaluated X" are the same X. And what stays untrusted on the agent path is the agent's PURPOSE claim — `rationale` — which reaches no check, no reason code, and no bound field; that is where the Case 2 demonstration now lives, and a test asserts the injected framing never enters the evidence bundle. **§4.2 Case 2 is amended to match**, following the D-015 precedent that prose left contradicting the implementation guarantees a future reader re-derives the fork from scratch. **Revisitable:** option (b) remains available and gets cheaper to adopt before the §9 step 8 corpus fossilises, per D-010's own reasoning about schema changes.

- **D-020 (2026-08-15) — The mandate's purpose fields are compared for EQUALITY, not as ceilings. Clarifies §5.7.** Ruled by John at the facilitated decision session, choosing option (a): *"Let's run with option a for this."* §5.1 gives `durationSeconds` and §5.7 says resource, beneficiary, duration and recurrence are "checked", but neither states the RELATION — which left open whether a purchase for LESS duration than authorised conforms. Both independent labellers (A-026a) labelled the under-duration fixture F016 as BLOCK and both reached it the same way — §5.1 names exactly one ceiling field, `maxNativeValueWei`, and `durationSeconds` carries no `max` prefix — while both flagged the "up to" reading as defensible and unsettled by the text. **The fact that decided it, measured rather than assumed:** `DemoPay.purchase` accepts any non-zero `msg.value` and grants exactly `durationSeconds`; **price does not scale with duration.** So the intuition behind a ceiling reading — that less privilege cannot harm the owner — is false here: a shorter duration at the same price is the owner paying in full for less access, and a longer one is the owner receiving more than they authorised. Neither direction is benign, which is what a ceiling reading would require. **Effect:** the implementation is confirmed unchanged, F016's BLOCK label stands, no re-label and no ablation re-run. **§5.7 is amended** to say the purpose fields are compared for equality and that only fields named `max*` are ceilings — following the D-015 precedent that prose left silent guarantees a future reader re-derives the fork. **Considered and rejected:** renaming the field to carry the rule in the schema. Correct in spirit, but it regenerates every EIP-712 type string and every fixture hash, and would invalidate the sample set the D-010 verifier was just built against — disproportionate to one sentence.

- **D-021 (2026-08-15) — A reverting simulation is a FAILED RULE and blocks. Clarifies §5.2.** Ruled by John at the facilitated decision session, choosing option (a): *"Let's go with path A."* §5.2 as amended by D-015 gives a clean dichotomy — a failed rule blocks, an unknown required check follows `failureMode` — and §5.7 lists "revert or success" as a supported effect, but nothing said which side a revert falls on. **This was the sole inter-labeller disagreement** (A-026b): labeller A read F002 as REVIEW, labeller B as BLOCK, and both independently wrote the ambiguity up as a spec gap before knowing of the other. It is why the D-011(c) rate is 10.0% rather than 0%. **Measured before the fork was put:** all four fixtures where the engine is stricter than labeller A — F002, F037, F038, F044 — block on `EVAL_SIMULATION_SUCCEEDS` and nothing else, so this was one question rather than two; undecodable calldata is already UNRESOLVED and does not cause the block. **Rationale for the ruling:** a revert is a determinate observed fact — the simulation succeeded in reporting that the call does not execute — so nothing is unknown, and §5.2's "unknown required check" does not describe it. **The counter-argument is recorded because it was not fully answered rather than because it lost:** §3.3(7) makes BLOCK remediable only by a new mandate or policy, so under this ruling a mis-set zero value costs the owner a whole new mandate rather than a corrected re-proposal. If field use makes that friction real, this is the entry to revisit, and option (b) — making the check UNRESOLVED so it follows `failureMode` — is a small code change. **Consequences, owed and not yet done:** labeller A's REVIEW labels on those four fixtures are wrong under the clarified spec and must be **re-labelled by fresh labellers, never edited by the build loop** (D-006), and the §7.3 ablation re-run afterwards.

- **D-022 (2026-08-15) — `reasonCodesHash` is documented in §5.4, and the D-010 verifier is extended to check it.** Ruled by John at the facilitated decision session, choosing options (a) and (c): *"let's follow your suggestion and go with a and c together."* **Context, because the finding shrank once measured.** The verifier reported this as its worst BLOCKER (`verifier/REPORT.md` F-3): `reasonCodesHash` unrecomputable by any third party, so a receipt carrying a substituted reason-code set would pass every check. It had in fact guessed the encoding correctly on its first family of attempts and could not match because **the receipt commits to the evaluator's codes UNIONED with the signer's own findings**, while `emit-samples.ts` had dropped the `reasonCodes` field the signer returns for exactly this purpose — a field whose own docstring reads "the exact ordered list that `reasonCodesHash` commits to, so a verifier can recompute it". A fixture omission cost an independent reviewer a blocking finding. Fixed and measured: all five samples now match `keccak256(utf8(sorted, deduped, newline-joined))`. **What was genuinely missing and is now ruled:** §5.4 documented neither the encoding nor the union semantics, and the union is the substantive half — the receipt's reason codes are NOT the evaluator's reason codes, which nobody reading §5 would guess. §5.4 is amended to state both. **Considered and rejected (option b):** committing to evaluator codes and signer findings as two separate hashed fields, so a reader can tell which layer said what without a side-channel list. Better design, but it changes the EIP-712 type string and regenerates every hash in the repository, and the audit trail is already recoverable from `signerFindings`, which the samples now publish. **Revisitable** if the audit trail proves awkward in the field.

- **D-023 (2026-08-15) — The EIP-712 type strings are published verbatim in a new §5.8, and an override sample is added. Closes the D-010 verifier's headline BLOCKER.** Ruled by John at the facilitated decision session, choosing option (b): *"I agree. Let's go with path B for this."* **The finding:** §5.1–§5.5 list bare field names, but EIP-712 needs the struct name and every field's Solidity type. §5 gave neither, so an independent implementer could not compute a single payload hash. The verifier recovered the receipt string by enumerating 3 struct names × 224 type combinations — **and the conventional uniform-`uint256` guess is in the set that failed.** It had a success oracle only because `domain.json` happens to publish `signerAddress`; without one, a wrong type string and an invalid signature are indistinguishable. **Why this was the most expensive gap:** in `abi.encode` every `uintN` occupies one left-padded word, so `uint16` and `uint256` encode identically and the width changes only the digest. There is no error and no diagnostic — the verifier reports a signer mismatch and a reasonable operator concludes the receipt is FORGED. **What recovering them bought:** before it, the CLI checked the signature and the evidence hash; after it, the whole chain — and a receipt correctly signed over the WRONG MANDATE would previously have passed, which is exactly the attack a conformance receipt exists to prevent. **Ruled:** §5.8 publishes all six strings (five payloads plus the domain), states that the payload hashes are bare EIP-712 `hashStruct` values with no `\x19\x01` prefix and no domain separator, and carries the warnings that cost the verifier real time — `uintN` width invisibility, `bytesN` right-padding, `callData` excluded from the struct, `signer` deliberately inside it, and EVM keccak256 rather than FIPS-202 SHA3-256. **`scripts/check-type-strings.sh` guards it** and is wired into the project gate: a PUBLISHED type string that has drifted from the code is worse than an absent one, because it is a confident wrong answer the implementer cannot detect. Verified to fail on drift and pass on restore. **Also ruled (the (b) half):** an override sample is added so §5.5 stops being the one payload nothing has ever verified, and the verifier's chain-binding concern is measured rather than reasoned. **Deliberately NOT taken (option c):** adding `chainId` and `vault` to `OverrideAuthorizationPayload`. The override references `mandateHash` and `policyHash`, which commit to chain and vault transitively, so the gap may be theoretical — that is what the sample is for, and a schema change on an untested hypothesis would be the wrong order.

- **D-024 (2026-08-15) — The `Verdict`, `FailureMode` and `Operation` enumerations are documented in a new §5.9, with the inversion warned about explicitly.** Ruled by John at the facilitated decision session, choosing option (a): *"Let's go with A then"*. **The finding (`verifier/REPORT.md` F-4), rated the most dangerous of the non-blockers:** the real mapping is `BLOCK = 0, REVIEW = 1, ALLOW = 2` — fail-closed, so an uninitialised or truncated field denies. That is already what the contracts and the signer do. **But every ordering in the prose implies the opposite**: §4.2 presents the cases allow-first, and §5.4 and §5.7 both say "allow, block, or review", so a reader deriving the enum from the document's own ordering gets `ALLOW = 0` — inverted, on the field §5.4 makes security-critical. **Why this outranks the other MAJORs:** `verdict` is a `uint8` inside the signed struct, so the reader's INTERPRETATION never affects the encoded bytes. A verifier holding it backwards passes the signature check, the evidence hash, and the mandate binding, then reports the opposite verdict. There is no failure mode in the cryptography that can surface it. The same unstated convention governs `failureMode` — which labeller B had to recover by cross-referencing fixture prose (A-026e), and which decides §4.2 Case 4's outcome — and `operation`. **Values verified against `contracts/src/types/SentinelTypes.sol` before publishing**, not transcribed from the report. **Considered and rejected (b):** renumbering so `ALLOW = 0` to match the prose. That destroys the fail-closed property, which is worth more than the consistency — a truncated verdict field would then mean allow. **Considered and rejected (c):** reordering §4.2 and §5.7 to present block-first. It removes the trap at its source, but §4.2's narrative ordering earns its place — a reader meets the mechanism through the conforming case — and a warning sentence buys the same safety without that cost.

- **D-025 (2026-08-15) — `PolicyPayload.allowedCallGraphHash` is RESERVED in v1, documented as not consulted. Clarifies §5.2 and §5.7.** Ruled by John at the facilitated decision session, choosing option (b): *"We'll go with B then."* **The finding (A-025a):** §5.2 declares the field and §5.7 lists "allowed top-level and internal call graph" among the supported checks, but the implementation never reads it — `EVAL_CALL_GRAPH_EXPECTED` compares against a hard-coded empty graph. **Not a defect today:** neither demo contract makes an internal call, so the empty graph IS the conforming one for both supported schemas and the check is correct on every fixture. It is a truthfulness-of-the-schema problem, the same shape as A-024. **Two live consequences, both now stated rather than silent:** a policy declaring any other call graph has no effect, so an owner could sign a constraint that does nothing; and §7.1's `unexpected-internal-call` class cannot be driven to a positive case in v1, so F051 demonstrates nothing and the ablation must not count it as a detection. **Considered and rejected (a) implement it:** it requires deciding what the hash commits to (ordered triples? a set? canonicalised how?) AND a demo contract that makes an internal call before it can be tested at all — real scope, and §12's kill criterion is explicit about scope expansion before the mechanism is proven. **Considered and rejected (c) remove the field:** cleanest schema, but it changes the `PolicyPayload` type string and regenerates every fixture and sample hash in the repository, for a field that would have to come back. **Honest framing for the S2 pack:** the call-graph check enforces an empty graph, which is correct for both supported schemas; the policy field that would generalise it is reserved and not yet consulted.

- **CLERICAL CORRECTION (2026-08-15).** D-019 through D-025 and A-023 through A-026 were originally written dated **2026-07-30**. That date was wrong — it was inferred from the previous session-state entry rather than checked, and the actual date of all of them is **2026-08-15**. Corrected in place across `docs/`, the proposal, and `verifier/REPORT.md`. Recorded rather than silently fixed because this decision log's value is that it can be audited, and a reader who had already cited the old dates needs to know they moved. The underlying rulings, their content, and their order are unchanged.

- **A-027 (2026-08-15) — The §5.4 wording written for D-022 was itself defective, and the independent verifier caught it on the first build from it.** D-022 ruled that §5.4 document the `reasonCodesHash` construction; the text I wrote to discharge that ruling had three defects, found when the verifier implemented from it — which is exactly the test the amended wording needed and would not have received from another reading by me. **(a) A published grammar that does not hold in the language D-010 mandates.** §5.4 printed `^[A-Za-z0-9_.:-]{1,64}$` and claimed it "removes the delimiter as a source of disagreement". **Verified empirically:** in Python `re.match` that pattern accepts `"EVAL_OK\n"`, because `$` also matches before a trailing newline; in Ruby `^`/`$` are line anchors unconditionally, so `"EVIL\nINJECTED"` passes. **The consequence is a hash collision, measured not reasoned:** `{"EVIL\nINJECTED"}` and `{"EVIL", "INJECTED"}` produce the byte-identical preimage and hash to `0x5850bebd…` — one receipt committing to two different sets. The grammar is the ONLY thing preventing it. The TypeScript signer is safe (JavaScript's `$` is end-of-input without `m`, verified), so this was a defect in the published text, not in the implementation — but D-010 mandates Python, so a spec-conformant Python verifier would have been vulnerable. Now printed unanchored with an explicit end-to-end requirement and a named hazard. **(b) "Union" read as a verifier instruction.** A verifier is handed `reasonCodes` already unioned; one that literally recomputed `union(reasonCodes, signerFindings)` would silently REPAIR the deletion of any code appearing in both. Every signer finding in the current samples also appears in `reasonCodes`, so **the corpus cannot distinguish the two readings** — both give identical hashes. §5.4 now says the verifier hashes the published array verbatim and asserts `signerFindings ⊆ reasonCodes` separately. **(c) Unstated failure behaviour**: a verifier must re-validate rather than trust the signer's boundary, and an absent `reasonCodes` on a signed receipt must fail rather than skip. **Also noted and owed:** no sample now has exactly one reason code, so nothing in the corpus pins the no-trailing-delimiter edge — a producer emitting `code + "\n"` for a single-element set would pass every shipped fixture. One fixture closes it. **The generalisable point, worth more than the fix:** documentation written to close a finding is itself unreviewed work, and the cheapest review of a spec amendment is somebody building from it.

- **D-026 (2026-08-15) — The EXECUTABILITY class is added to the specification and the evaluator, and §3.3(7)'s remedy clause is narrowed.** Ruled by John at the facilitated decision session: *"I accept your recommendation for all three."* **The gap, found independently by three labellers:** §5.2's dichotomy is failed-rule → BLOCK, unknown-check → `failureMode`, and neither describes a fact that is KNOWN, ADVERSE, and says nothing about the mandate — a paused vault (F057), a rotated signer (F035), a value above the vault's own hard cap (F007). Labeller C routed them to BLOCK by elimination and then noticed §3.3(7) prescribes the wrong remedy. **The real defect was in §3.3(7)**, which welded two claims together: *a block cannot be overridden* (a security property worth keeping) and *a block requires a new mandate or policy* (false for all three — the remedies are unpause, use the current signer, propose less). Amended to keep the first and narrow the second. **The concept was already ratified and simply lived in one layer.** A-011 built exactly this distinction into the isolated signer as its EXECUTABILITY severity tier — "EXECUTABILITY is only for facts that make the receipt unusable" — and D-012 ratified it; the specification and the conformance engine had never heard of it. `EVAL_EXECUTABILITY_CODES` now declares the set (`EVAL_VAULT_NOT_PAUSED`, `EVAL_NONCE_CURRENT`, `EVAL_VALUE_WITHIN_VAULT_CAP`, `EVAL_ACTION_DEADLINE`), guarded structurally against naming a code the engine does not declare and against overlapping the mandate-conformance set — an overlap would make the remedy ambiguous, which is the whole point of the classification. **No verdict changes, no schema change, no receipt hash changes, no re-labelling.** The bundle already carries the codes; what becomes derivable is the REMEDY. **On F007:** it stays BLOCK, as an executability block — which is why labeller C could rate ALLOW defensible and still not be right. Also recorded: the vault's hard cap is a THIRD ceiling, independent of the mandate-and-policy intersection §5.2 describes.

- **D-027 (2026-08-15) — §5.7's code-hash line is qualified to match D-015. Clerical alignment, not a new ruling.** Ruled by John in the same session. D-015 already settled that a target code-hash mismatch is UNRESOLVED rather than a VIOLATION, quoting §4.2 Case 4's "Sentinel does not label the target malicious. It reports insufficient evidence for automatic approval", and the implementation follows it. What labeller D caught is that **§5.7 still contradicted it**, listing "exact … code hash" among the deterministic equality checks, which reads as a failed rule. Qualified so the code-hash comparison is described as an EVIDENCE check following `failureMode`, with the other items on that line left as equality checks. Same shape as D-015's own §5.2 amendment: prose left contradicting a ruling guarantees a future reader re-derives the fork.

- **D-028 (2026-08-15) — Undecodable calldata is an evidence gap and follows `failureMode`; the apparent asymmetry is named rather than removed.** Ruled by John in the same session, confirming the implementation. §3.3(8) forbids an automatic allow on undecodable calldata but never said which side of §5.2's dichotomy it falls on. It is UNRESOLVED: reviews under REVIEW, blocks under FAIL_CLOSED, and §3.3(8) is satisfied either way. **The asymmetry labeller C flagged is not undecodability behaving two ways:** a call too malformed to carry a usable selector also fails a determinate check — the selector is not on the vault's allowlist — and blocks on THAT, while a call that is merely non-canonical but still executable reviews. Two reasons co-occurring in one fixture class. §5.7 now says so, because a reader who spots the pattern without the explanation will reasonably read it as inconsistency. **The counter-argument is recorded rather than dismissed:** A-017 establishes that trailing bytes past the declared arguments are the single case where Sentinel is stricter than the EVM, so under REVIEW an owner may override something Sentinel deliberately refused to parse. Making undecodability a failed rule remains available; it would cost a re-label of the malformed-calldata class.

- **A-028 (2026-08-15) — Four independent adversarial reviews at fixed commit `9059346`. ONE LIVE CODE DEFECT, in Gate S1's signed scope, plus substantial honesty and coverage debt. NOTHING HAS BEEN REMEDIATED YET.** Run under D-017's conditions: four fresh-context lenses (step 7, the corpus and labelling pipeline, the ablation, and steps 1–3), all four confirming `git rev-parse HEAD` before starting, all reading the actual implementation and tests rather than the evidence summaries, and the tree frozen and verified clean throughout. Artifacts preserved under `docs/review-2026-08-15/artifacts/` because the reviewers' scratch was session-scoped and would have been lost.

  **THE DEFECT — F1, live, reproduced end to end, and it defeats a ratified ruling against the threat that ruling names.** `ts/src/signer/attest.ts` `checkEvidenceDecoding`: when a bundle claims `decoded:"false"` with a `failureCode` in `TARGET_BINDING_FAILURES`, the function returns `[]` **unconditionally**, never consulting whether the signer's own `decodeBySelector` succeeded. Any composer of an `evaluateAndSign` request therefore switches the entire D-014 attestation off with a two-field lie. The reviewer ran it against a real Anvil, vault and signer process: a wrong-resource purchase was signed ALLOW, executed, and wrote `entitlementExpiry[owner][premium-monthly]` onchain, with the receipt's `evidenceHash` committing to a bundle carrying **no decoded parameters at all**. D-014's stated justification — "the bundle's decoded parameters become signer-attested rather than evaluator-asserted, so a wrong-purpose ALLOW is detectable after the fact" — does not hold against a compromised evaluator, which is the exact party D-014 names. **This is inside Gate S1's signed scope**, and it is in the same code path whose earlier defect the D-017 review caught: the fix for that defect introduced this one. **The project's own mutation harness has a mutation NAMED for this hole** (`V2 "treat decoded:false as a blanket escape hatch"`) that mutates a different branch and has been reporting caught throughout; the reviewer's own widening mutation survived the full suite. **No remedy is possible without a design decision:** the signer genuinely cannot refuse here without re-breaking D-017, and recording a non-blocking marker requires a new severity tier, since every code in `REASON_SEVERITY` blocks ALLOW by construction. Flagged to John, not patched.

  **Coverage debt on steps 1–3: 29 of 45 mutations survived a fully green 43-Foundry + 322-TypeScript run**, with 9 of 9 controls caught so the instrument is validated in both directions. §3.3(9)'s "nonce consumed before the external call" is verified by nothing — the reentrancy guard masks it, so two defences presented as independent are not independently tested. The vault's `allowedSelector` backstop, the `WrongVault` check, the zero-address guards, and the entire `executeWithOverride` path are uncovered; `executeWithOverride` is absent from the invariant campaign's handler set although §3.3 names its property. Essentially every A-016 hardening fix — socket mode, the TOCTOU, backpressure, the two-signers-one-key shutdown bug, odd-length hex — shipped **in code and comments without regression tests**, so A-016's conclusions are defended by its own prose, which is the position D-017 exists to prevent. **`scripts/mutate.sh` is TypeScript-only**, and that gap is where most of this class lives.

  **Defects in work done THIS session, all mine.** (a) `propose.test.ts`'s boundary test **cannot fail**: its loop `continue`s on every one of the seven TRIGGERS, so the three assertions carrying the published claim execute **zero times** — the fourth occurrence of this exact pattern in the project, and the first I authored. (b) The claim that test guarded is **false**: a 4-byte selector collision inside the transcriber's own grammar (`fUXSEz2ajwh(bytes32)` → `0xc188528b`, found in 106 seconds) makes §7.1's malformed-calldata class proposal-reachable. It fails closed, so no wrong ALLOW. (c) **I contaminated the labels of record.** My re-label brief handed labeller C D-025, which is implementation-derived behaviour, and D-011(b) forbids it; C's own note says F051 "turns entirely on D-025" and the independent reading would have gone the other way. (d) `assertNoLeakage` matches `"key"` with both quotes, so every denylist entry falls to a one-word prefix (`engineVerdict`, `failingChecks`, `l3Verdict` all pass) — **and the gap is already in use**: six labelling views carry the decoder's internal enum strings, which labeller C quotes verbatim. This is the same "denylist entry that cannot fire" defect I had found and believed fixed one commit earlier; I fixed the case-sensitivity and missed the match shape.

  **Ablation overstatements.** `addedByL3` is computed as `L3 \ L2` rather than `L3 \ (L1 ∪ L2)`, so F056 is counted in both "caught by the baseline alone" and "added by mandate conformance" — 19, not 20. The layer partition is **non-monotone**: `MANDATE_CONFORMANCE_CODES` strips `EVAL_TARGET_BOUND` and `EVAL_SELECTOR_BOUND`, leaving L2 with no target constraint while the strictly weaker L1 has an allowlist. `EVAL_CALLDATA_BINDING` is signed-payload integrity, not mandate purpose, and its misclassification makes F019 a signature-binding catch wearing a mandate-conformance badge. Defensible contribution is **17–19**. The latency column measures the harness — L2 is timed as L3's work plus a Set lookup per check, which is why L2 reads slower than L3 on 49 of 50 fixtures — and excludes simulation without saying so. §7.2's own caveat ("not evidence that current vendors miss Case 3") appears nowhere in the report, and the absence of the second baseline class is undisclosed. `malicious-retrieved-instructions` is **vacuous**: `FixtureSpec` has no rationale field, so F049 and F050 are byte-identical to F009 and F012 apart from mandate identity, and both are still counted.

  **My 10.0% → 0.0% framing was wrong and is withdrawn.** Both the corpus and ablation reviewers independently established that labeller D received the **same ten fixture ids** as labeller B rather than a fresh draw, and that the sole disagreement's subject had been ruled on by D-021 before the re-label. The rate confirms the fix; it evidences nothing about corpus quality, and the commit message calling it "the clearest evidence yet that the amendments did what they were ruled to do" overclaimed. **The genuine robustness result, which is better and was invisible:** re-scoring everything against the superseded label set gives **identical** false-allow numbers (38/19/1). Only exact-match moved.

  **What HELD, re-attacked from scratch with different probes and a different rig.** A-005 signer isolation held completely — 26 method names refused, raw `__proto__` on the wire, a 9 MiB line destroying the connection, socket mode `0600` and its directory `0700` verified by `statSync`, no signing path but `evaluateAndSign`, no route to the key. A-013's differential independence is real and the control mutation died immediately. A-012/D-013's wording carries no overclaim. **D-014's boundary is exactly where D-014 says it is** — confirmed by signing a truthful wrong-target bundle, and correctly NOT reported as a bug. Reentrancy genuinely bites. The D-011(a) freeze is real: one commit, thirteen minutes before the first fixture, never re-pinned. The 20% sample selection is unsteerable, recomputed independently. The time fixtures are not vacuous, and labeller C reconstructed chain time from `expiryAfter` rather than trusting the prose — the strongest evidence in the corpus that the labeller/evaluator split has substance.

  **Verdict, stated plainly: none of these produces a wrong ALLOW except F1, and every other finding is an honesty defect — a claim stronger than its evidence.** For a project whose thesis is that a green suite is evidence only for what it exercised, that is the failure mode that matters most.

- **D-029 (2026-08-15) — A failed rule takes precedence over an unresolved check. Clarifies §5.2.** Ruled by John at the facilitated decision session: *"Agreed, we'll go with A for this."* §5.2 said "Any failed rule blocks" and "Any unknown required check is governed by `failureMode`" without saying what happens when BOTH hold, and labeller E — working only from the spec — resolved it from sentence ORDERING and flagged that as an inference it should not have had to make. `verdictOf` tests VIOLATION first, so E's reading matched the implementation. **It decides real fixtures:** F038 trips `EVAL_CALLDATA_UNDECODABLE` (unresolved, from dirty address bits) AND `EVAL_SIMULATION_SUCCEEDS` (a failed rule under D-021) simultaneously; F046 is the same shape. **The stakes are asymmetric:** §3.3(8) is satisfied either way since neither yields an automatic allow, but §3.3(7) makes BLOCK non-overridable while REVIEW is executable with an owner override — so the precedence choice decides whether the owner keeps agency over these actions. **Rationale:** an unresolved check is an ABSENCE of information, and an absence cannot undo a positive finding. Learning that something else is unknown does not make a known violation less certain; in F038 the revert is determinate and is not softened by the decoder also having refused the bytes. **The condition that would reverse this**, recorded so it is checkable rather than rhetorical: if failures were being RECORDED as VIOLATION when they are really "could not establish", precedence would be laundering uncertainty into certainty. That would be a misclassification to fix at the check level, not a reason to weaken precedence — and D-027 and D-028 moving two such checks into the unresolved column is evidence the classification is being maintained. **Considered and rejected:** splitting by whether the unresolved check is causally related to the failure. More faithful in principle, but it needs a relation the evaluator can compute, and encoding a judgement call as a rule is what has bitten hardest at this layer.

- **D-030 (2026-08-15) — "Conflicting state" is a FAILED RULE, not an evidence gap. Clarifies §3.3(8).** Ruled by John at the facilitated decision session: *"A with your caveat for this gap."* §3.3(8) lists six conditions that "never produce automatic allow" — a floor, not a verdict. Four have since been resolved individually (D-015 code-identity as unresolved, D-028 undecodable calldata as unresolved, D-021 a revert as a failed rule, D-026 the executability carve-out); **"conflicting state" never was**, and it is the one with no natural home. **The fixture:** F024's mandate names a `principal` that is not the vault's owner, and the vault has that mandate ACTIVE — so it posits a vault where an activation §5.1 forbids has already happened. Both clean labellers (E and F, independently, neither having read the implementation) labelled it BLOCK and both marked it their lowest-confidence call, giving the same two readings. **Rationale for the ruling:** both operands are KNOWN and the equality FAILS. Nothing is unestablished, and calling it an evidence gap would describe a determinate contradiction as missing information — the same argument as D-029. **THE CAVEAT, RECORDED AT JOHN'S DIRECTION BECAUSE THE REASONING IS NOT FULLY COMFORTABLE.** Labeller F identified the weakness precisely: the vault reaching this state at all means something upstream already went wrong, so "the mandate is active but was never activatable" is arguably a SYSTEM inconsistency rather than a property of the action being judged. And the precedent now points the other way — a reader who sees two other members of the same §3.3(8) list routed to `failureMode` by D-027 and D-028 will reasonably expect the third to follow. **The honest version of the objection is option (c):** classify per condition rather than treating "conflicting state" as one thing, since an un-activatable active mandate is a different animal from an RPC that disagrees with itself. Not taken, because it costs a taxonomy the evaluator must maintain and the project already carries two — but it is the entry to revisit if field use produces a conflicting-state case that is genuinely about missing evidence rather than a determinate contradiction.

- **D-031 (2026-08-15) — §5.7's enumeration of supported checks is reconciled against the engine and mechanically guarded.** Ruled by John at the facilitated decision session: *"B with your caveat for this gap."* **The finding:** labeller E, working only from the specification, noticed that §5.7 never names the check that the mandate's embedded `policyHash` is the vault's active policy, and drew the consequence unprompted — an evaluator built from §5.7 alone omits it, and F025 then ALLOWs. The check exists (`EVAL_MANDATE_BINDS_POLICY`, and F025 blocks on it alone), so this was a documentation gap — but **it fails OPEN**, which is the D-010 shape exactly. **Measured rather than assumed:** the engine declares 41 checks and ELEVEN had no home in §5.7's prose, including all three binding checks §3.3(4) and §3.3(5) rest on. **Ruled:** a new §5.7.1 documents all 41, and `scripts/check-eval-codes.sh` fails the project gate if a check exists in the engine and not there. Verified to fail on an added-but-undocumented code and to pass on restore. **THE CAVEAT, RECORDED AT JOHN'S DIRECTION.** The guard asserts COVERAGE, not correctness — that someone declared where a check belongs, not that the description is right. It is strictly weaker than `check-type-strings.sh`, which compares two byte-exact strings, and §5.7.1 says so rather than glossing it. It is still worth having: this was the third spec-versus-implementation drift found by someone outside the build loop, and a guard is the only thing that stops a fourth. **Considered and rejected:** publishing `EVAL_CODES` itself as the normative list. Maximum precision, but it welds the specification to the implementation's naming and would make reimplementation a transcription exercise — destroying the exact signal D-010 exists to produce, which is an independent implementer deriving behaviour from a written schema and FAILING where the schema is thin. §5.7.1 therefore carries the identifiers for auditability while stating that they are not normative.

- **A-044 (2026-08-16) — The six remaining §9 step 3 findings, all ruled and all fixed. Every one lived in code carrying a comment that claimed the property it did not have.** Ruled by John one at a time; each verified before it was put to him rather than relayed from the reviewer. **(a) THE BACKPRESSURE REPAIR BOUNDED NOTHING, and a false comment about it was cited as evidence in A-016.** `socket.pause()` was called INSIDE the drain loop — pausing stops future READS, not the loop dispatching lines already in the buffer — so one sub-MiB write of 25,000 lines dispatched all of them (803 MB RSS from one connection). And both caps were per-connection with nothing bounding connections (1.8 GB from 40 compliant sockets). **The file header asserted "a local caller cannot grow the signer's memory without bound" and a comment beside the code said it existed to make that header claim "true rather than aspirational". It was false when written.** Fixed: the cap is tested BEFORE each dispatch, `settle` nudges the drain loop on resume so a paused connection cannot strand buffered lines, and `MAX_CONNECTIONS` refuses excess sockets. The header now states the three caps as what is enforced rather than asserting an absence of bounds. **(b) THE SIGNER'S REASON-CODE NAMESPACE WAS CALLER-WRITABLE.** `reasonCodesHash` commits to the union of the caller's codes and the signer's findings, `signerFindings` is NOT a field of the signed §5.4 payload, and nothing rejected a caller-supplied `SIGNER_` prefix. A reviewer signed an ALLOW whose committed codes asserted the signer had found the vault paused, the mandate inactive and itself not the active signer — while the signer had found nothing. Verdicts were unaffected, which is precisely why this is an integrity defect in the RECORD rather than a bypass: an authenticated receipt could carry statements the signer never made. Rejected as the fix: adding `signerFindings` to the signed payload, which is more informative but changes a §5.4 schema the corpus and verifier are both built against, landing it in the re-label batch. **(c) `evidenceHash` WAS NOT INJECTIVE.** `stringToBytes` replaces every unpaired surrogate with U+FFFD, so distinct bundles hash identically — verified directly: `"x\ud800y"` and `"x\udbffy"` both encode to `78efbfbd79`. All 2,048 lone surrogates collapse to one sequence. **The asymmetry mattered as much as the collision:** the D-010 verifier had been taught the same morning to reject these under RFC 8785 §3.2.2.2, so the signer was signing evidence the independent verifier structurally could not reproduce — a genuine receipt reading as FAILED. Both sides now agree on what is signable. Rejected: requiring `evidenceCanonical` to BE canonical by re-canonicalising, which closes more but couples the signer to the evaluator's canonicaliser, a remit question D-014 keeps separate. **(d) AND (e) TWO PATHS PRODUCED A REFUSAL WITH NO SIGNED ARTIFACT — in the same session that published §5.5.1 to close exactly that gap.** `chainId`, `actionNonce`, `valueWei` and `maxNativeValueWei` used the unbounded `uint()` though `bounded()` existed and was used elsewhere; a value ≥ 2^256 parsed, then `leftPad` threw an ordinary Error — classified `SIGNER_ERROR` rather than `BAD_REQUEST`, and thrown BEFORE `refuse()` could sign anything. And the `attributable = false` carve-out suppressed the D-012 record for all four structural findings though its own stated rationale — "a payload contradicting its own calldata describes no action the signer could say it refused" — describes `SIGNER_DATAHASH_MISMATCH` alone. For a wrong vault or chain, `hashAction` succeeds and the action is perfectly nameable, so **Gate S2 could not prove by signed artifact that the signer refuses a wrong-vault request.** Both fixed. **(f) THE SIMULATION ANCHOR IS RECORDED AS A DECLARED LIMIT, NOT FIXED.** `blockHashAt` checks the block exists and matches; there is no recency bound, and a reviewer signed a receipt anchored at genesis while head was block 7. §8's claim is literally true — conformance IS established against simulated effects at a recorded block — but WHICH block is bounded only by the evaluator's honesty, and the evaluator is the untrusted party. Ruled a limit rather than a bug because a recency bound is a policy parameter nobody has reasoned about, and inventing one at the end of a long session is how a number nobody can defend enters a security check. **VERIFICATION, AND THE PART WORTH KEEPING.** 21 new tests. Against the unfixed code, 12 of the first 18 fail and the 6 that pass are the controls. **But the first backpressure test I wrote passed against the unfixed server** — it pinned the deadlock my own repair could introduce, not the defect it named. I checked, found it, and added a third test that measures peak concurrency with a counting chain reader: **200 against a cap of 16 on the old drain loop, ≤16 on the new one.** A regression test that cannot fail on the defect it names is the exact shape this project keeps finding, and I shipped one for twenty minutes. Suite 384 → 405.

- **A-043 (2026-08-16) — CRITICAL, DEMONSTRATED, AND FIXED: an untrusted evaluator could obtain a signed ALLOW for calldata nobody decoded, and execute it onchain. A-028 F1's repair closed one of two branches.** Found by the D-044(b) review of §9 step 3 — the review this session nearly did not run. **The defect.** `checkEvidenceDecoding` has two returns under `claimed.decoded === "false"`. A-028 F1 hardened the first (`TARGET_BINDING_FAILURES`) with `requestedVerdict === "ALLOW" ? [MISMATCH] : []`. The second still read `return mine.ok ? [MISMATCH] : []` — **returning NO finding for an ALLOW whenever the signer's own decode also failed.** `ts/src/decode/abi.ts` states in its own header that Solidity ignores calldata past the declared parameters, so **appending 32 bytes makes a call the EVM executes normally and this decoder refuses.** Claim `decoded:"false"` with a non-target `failureCode`, and both sides "agree" the bytes are unreadable. **REPRODUCED END TO END, twice, against the real socket and the compiled vault: a wrong-resource purchase landed onchain (mandated entitlement 0, premium entitlement created), and separately `approve(attacker, type(uint256).max)` was signed and executed — the flagship Case 2 attack that D-042(b) records has no vault backstop behind it.** Both under receipts whose `evidenceHash` committed to a bundle carrying no parameters at all, with `signerFindings: []`. The attacker is exactly the compromised evaluator D-014 names as the threat. **THE FIX** hoists the ALLOW guard above the failure-code test: any `decoded:"false"` + ALLOW is refused. **The original justification was always branch-independent** — `decode.ok === false` makes `EVAL_CALLDATA_UNDECODABLE` UNRESOLVED for ANY failure code and `verdictOf` returns ALLOW only when nothing is unresolved (§3.3(8)), so no honest evaluator emits the pair. **A-028's repair generalised the DEMONSTRATION rather than the ARGUMENT, which is why a second reviewer found the other half.** D-017 is not re-broken: BLOCK and REVIEW fall through untouched, and four tests pin that. **WHY NOTHING CAUGHT IT, AND THIS IS THE PART TO REMEMBER.** The A-028 regression tests all pair a lying bundle with DECODABLE calldata, so only the `mine.ok === true` half was ever exercised. The comment above their fixture names the other state in terms — *"an unsupported selector, against which the signer's own decode fails too, so bundle and signer honestly agree and nothing fires"* — and treats it as a fixture hazard to route around. **That sentence describes the vulnerability and files it as a testing inconvenience.** `scripts/mutate.sh` V2 mutates the same reachable half and is caught; the unreachable half was touched by no test and no mutation, and **380/380 passed with the hole live.** **ELEVEN TESTS WERE PASSING THROUGH THE HOLE.** They went red the instant it closed — the only reason the blast radius was visible. Unit fixtures had reached for an arbitrary unsupported selector (`0xdeadbeef…`, `0xa1b2c3d4…`) whenever they needed "some calldata" for a test about the nonce guard, the reason-code table, or fail-closed behaviour; that made the signer's own decode fail, which made the stub honestly report `decoded:"false"`, which made the signer return nothing for an ALLOW. **The shared fixture default for the entire unit suite ran through the bypass.** Repaired with `decodablePurchaseCallData()` in the harness and a matching `SELECTOR`; tests genuinely about undecodable calldata now say so with their own bytes. **VERIFICATION:** both reproductions re-run against the patched signer and both are REFUSED; two new regression tests fail against the pre-fix code and pass after, with nothing else moving — the fix is exactly scoped. Suite 380 → 384. **The A-016 spend limit that left this component's skeptic verifications unrun was recorded, disclosed in §11, and signed over at S2. It hid a critical bypass of the only key-holding component for the length of that gap.**

- **D-044 (2026-08-16) — Session close: push, one last review, both deferrals confirmed, and STOP. Five rulings.** Ruled by John at the facilitated session that closed the day. **(a) PUSHED.** 14 commits including the S2 signature existed only on this disk; the branch is now at `1a27a36`, verified against the remote rather than trusting the push output. **The distinction recorded in `session-state.md`'s header matters more than the act: pushing to a private remote is BACKUP, not publication.** D-016 governs publication, `check-rename-gate.sh` verifies the repository is private on every gate run, and a future instance must not read "pushed" as any relaxation of that. **(b) ONE ADVERSARIAL REVIEW OF §9 STEP 3, the last open item in the S2 pack's §11.** The isolated signer HAS been reviewed — A-016 ran five fresh-context reviewers and the core isolation claims held under real attack — but **6 of its 8 skeptic verifications never executed** when the account hit a spend limit, so most findings were adjudicated by the build loop against its own code, which A-016 itself calls "exactly the self-grading the process exists to avoid". The reviewer is briefed to treat A-016's verdicts as **unverified assertions to attack rather than established facts**, and to try to un-refute what it refuted. Ruled on today's base rate — three reviews, roughly forty findings, both new guards defeatable — and because the signer holds the only key, so an unadjudicated finding costs most there. **One round, not five: review rounds run forever if nobody says stop.** Rejected: re-adjudicating A-016's six findings alone, which can only re-examine what A-016 already thought to look for — A-028 recorded that a mutation set written by the implementer inherits its author's blind spots even when the author is a reviewer. **(c) THE TOKEN CAP DEFERRAL STANDS (confirms D-042(b)).** Nothing material changed except that the gap is now impossible to lose: `test_LIMIT_vaultCapsNativeValueOnlyAndNotTokenAuthority` asserts the limit and fails the moment a cap lands, §7.1 states it, the register tracks it. The original reasons hold — net-new capability, D-003's stop condition, and that a vault-side cap would partly mask what the harness exists to measure. **Revisiting a sound ruling because the work is available is how scope expands after a signed gate, and that is the reason recorded rather than the convenience.** **(d) RECEIPT EPOCH BINDING DEFERRED, batched with the re-label.** The case is stronger than for the cap: it touches the signer AND the payload schema, so it is the item most likely to move the corpus, which makes it the one that genuinely belongs WITH a re-label rather than before one. Both directions are pinned by tests and the comment that misdescribed the behaviour is corrected. **The asymmetry recorded because it argues the other way: unlike the cap, this gap has an OPERATIONAL trigger rather than an adversarial one** — an incident rollback or a re-provisioned key — and "rotation revokes" is what an operator will assume. That is the argument for building it, and it lost to the schema coupling rather than being unanswered. **(e) STOP HERE. Pre-publication is not started.** With both capabilities deferred and the register batched behind the re-label trigger, pre-publication is the only remaining direction, and starting it fires the trigger: all five corpus defects, a re-label of all 50 fixtures, the dashboard, and Gate 8 with John's five held questions. **Ruled to stop at a clean point instead** — everything green, pushed, and recorded; both mid-build gates signed; every deferred item pinned by a test or a named trigger. **The stated reason is pace: today moved an enormous amount, and starting the largest remaining programme in the same session that signed S2 is how a day's findings get skimmed rather than read.** Rejected: building the evidence dashboard as groundwork, which needs no ruling from John and is a Gate 8 prerequisite — but is net-new capability and would have reversed D-043's "open no new front" hours after it was ruled, making it the third deferral overturned in one session.

- **A-042 (2026-08-16) — THE D-010 EXPERIMENT, RUN PROPERLY FOR THE FIRST TIME: an independent implementation was built from §5.5.1 alone and pointed at a real signed refusal it had never seen. Everything the section STATED matched on the first run. Everything it FAILED TO STATE diverged.** The two halves were deliberately kept apart and **the interpretation was declared before either finished**: if the independently-built verifier accepted the artifact, §5.5.1 was precise enough to build from; if it rejected it, that was a specification gap found the way D-010 is designed to find one, and the more valuable outcome. **The result was the second, and specific.** A schema-only agent (allowlist `verifier/**` + the proposal, denied `ts/**` and `contracts/**`) implemented refusal verification; separately the real signer emitted `fixtures/samples/refusal-vault-paused`, the first artifact in the repository carrying a §5.5.1 record. **WHAT MATCHED FIRST TIME: the field list, the field order, the domain tag, the preimage bytes, the charset rules, and the signature construction** — raw ECDSA over the digest, which §5.5.1 never stated and the agent inferred correctly, reporting it as a guess rather than a fact. **WHAT DIVERGED — THE ENVELOPE, which §5.5.1 left entirely unspecified.** It said "SignedRefusalRecord contains RefusalRecord plus `signerSignature`" and named no file, no key, and no nesting depth. The agent guessed three shapes; the corpus uses a fourth — `receipt.json → refusalRecord → {record, signature, reasonCodes}`, one level deeper, with the member spelled `signature` rather than the `signerSignature` §5.5.1 itself named. **THREE FURTHER DEFECTS IN §5.5.1, ALL MINE, ALL FOUND BY THE INDEPENDENT SIDE, ALL VERIFIED DIRECTLY BEFORE BEING ACCEPTED.** (1) **`actionHash` does not attribute a refusal**, and the counterexample is in the repository: `refusal-vault-paused` and `case-1-allow` carry a BYTE-IDENTICAL `actionHash` (`0x6f630833…`), because the same action was legitimately decided twice — evaluated ALLOW against an unpaused vault, then refused when the state changed. §5.5.1 listed `actionHash` and `evidenceHash` without saying either must be compared to anything, so a genuine signed refusal could be moved onto a different bundle and caught only by `evidenceHash`. (2) **The injectivity argument was wrong in both directions.** I credited it to the charsets alone; precisely, fixed ARITY prevents a malformed record colliding with a well-formed one and the CHARSETS prevent two malformed records colliding with each other. Confirmed by construction: `requestedVerdict = "BLOCK\nSMUGGLED"` with `reasonCodesHash = "H"` joins to the same preimage as `"BLOCK"` with `"SMUGGLED\nH"`, so one signature attests to two distinct records — **D-022's reason-code delimiter smuggling, in a second place, reintroduced by me on the day I wrote the section.** (3) **`signer` is self-declared**, so a verifier that does not compare the recovered address against the deployment's known signer will certify a record anyone can mint. §5.5.1 is amended for all four. **AN OBSERVATION ABOUT THE AGENT'S CONDUCT THAT IS ITSELF EVIDENCE:** the refusal fixture appeared in its allowlist mid-session, after its brief. It did not read the file — it ran its verifier at it and treated the output as the measurement, which is the only handling that preserves the experiment. **ALSO FOUND, unprompted:** two `--tamper` modes were VACUOUS on a refusal bundle — they mutated a receipt body that does not exist and reported "correctly rejected" — which nothing in the corpus could have exposed until a refusal artifact existed; they now raise `NotApplicable`. A refusal carries no expiry, `schemaVersion` is cross-checked against nothing, and any human-readable `refusalReason` is outside the signature and therefore is not evidence. Tests 101 → 146; 7/7 samples verify; 55/55 applicable tamper cases. **Three test-file changes, none weakening an assertion:** the sample-walking helper was split because seven tests asserted a §5.4 receipt property over every sample and the corpus now contains a bundle with no receipt by construction; the two vacuous tamper modes; and one check's NAME widened to cite §5.5.1 alongside §5.4, kept substring-compatible so the four tests asserting on it needed no edit.

- **D-043 (2026-08-16) — Post-S2 direction: CONSOLIDATE. No new front, no ladder rung. Plus four rulings clearing the immediate queue.** Ruled by John at the facilitated session. **(a) DIRECTION — work the v1.1 register down and open nothing new.** §14.8's ladder is open now that S2 is signed: rung 1 (the receipt-verifier CLI) already shipped inside v1 as D-010, rung 2 (executed vendor comparisons) is available, and the 14.3 attestation stretch is eligible because the §7.5 gates are green. **All declined for now.** The reasoning: one day produced 25+ findings across two guards, a signed gate's own deliverable, and the vault's published claims, and the marginal value of closing that out exceeds anything new. **Rung 2 was declined on a sharper ground and it is worth recording: it directly reverses D-001, and it would require unwinding D-008(2)'s empty-column condition and the vendor-honesty guard — putting executed-comparison claims into a project whose entire honesty apparatus was built on their absence.** Publication was declined as premature rather than wrong; it becomes the natural sequel once the register is clear. **(b) THE RE-LABEL IS BOUND TO PRE-PUBLICATION WITH A NAMED TRIGGER.** Five corpus defects can only be fixed with it — the `conflicting-block-state` GAP, F032's expiry isolation, the F026/F051 call-graph-hash contradiction, F056's reentrancy misfiling, and the corpus vault config. **A precision that changes the cost and had been over-stated: a re-label does NOT require a new frozen prompt.** D-035 ruled the prompt is not at fault, so `check-label-prompt.sh` stays green and its hash is untouched; the cost is fresh labeller runs over corrected fixtures, replacing E and F as the labels of record, and re-running the §7.3 ablation. **RULED: no re-label now; it becomes a hard pre-publication condition alongside Gate 8 and D-016's rename gate, triggered by any move toward publication, with all five defects fixed together and all 50 re-labelled under the SAME frozen prompt.** Rejected: doing it now as the v1.1 anchor (largest single piece of work outstanding, and it would replace the labels of record and the disagreement figure the S2 pack cites, forcing every number there to be re-verified), and never re-labelling (§7.1's class table would permanently overstate what the corpus exercises and the GAP would never get its fixture). **The named trigger exists because pre-publication is where deferred things go quiet — that is the third time this session that risk has been named, and it is now a condition rather than an intention.** **(c) THE §5 REFUSAL RECORD IS PUBLISHED NOW.** Not deferred, because it is a documentation correction rather than new capability: the signer already produces a signed `RefusalRecord` with a fixed field order and a documented digest, and `ts/src/signer/eip712.ts` carries the comment *"the D-010 verifier will need to check these, and a refusal nobody else can verify is not evidence."* **The implementation anticipated the exact failure, wrote it down, and it happened anyway — because nobody published the format to the one document the verifier is permitted to read.** Publishing it lets a fresh schema-only agent implement verification the way D-010 intends. **(d) THE OVERRIDE AUTHORIZATION EVENT IS ADDED NOW; the token cap and receipt epoch binding stay at v1.1.** §3.3(2) requires override be "separately authenticated, unavailable to the agent, and **logged**", and it is not logged — `ActionExecuted` carries only `viaOverride` and the receipt's `decisionId`, while every other item in §3.3(2) has a dedicated event. **That is an unmet stated invariant rather than a missing feature**, which is the same category as the comment correction and a different category from the token cap. The token cap stays deferred per D-042(b) — selecting it would have been a reversal of that ruling rather than a continuation — and epoch binding stays deferred as the largest of the three, touching the signer and the payload schema, best batched with the re-label. **(e) The workspace machine-state guard's seven `evm_key` findings are re-baselined.** Anvil's published dev keys in `ts/src/corpus/run.ts` and `ts/src/tools/emit-samples.ts`, verified by derivation: `0xac0974be…` yields `0xf39Fd6e51aad88F6F4ce6aB8827279cffFb92266`, Anvil account #0 and the owner address throughout the fixtures. Baseline entries key on (rule, file, matched text), so this accepts seven exact lines and a genuinely new key in the same file still fires — and it extends a position the baseline already took for the identical keys in `ts/test/harness.ts` and `ts/src/tools/sample-check.ts`. Rejected: allowlisting the key values in shared workspace tooling (a real loosening rather than an acceptance of specific lines), and leaving the guard red (a guard that is always red is one nobody reads — this project's own repeated finding — and it would mask a genuine leak in the noise).

- **A-041 (2026-08-16) — D-042(d) executed: the D-010 verifier is repaired by an agent that never read the implementation, and the repair's most valuable output is a SPECIFICATION finding, not a code fix. 70 tests → 101.** Given the published schemas, the samples, and the five defects stated as symptoms; denied `ts/**`, `contracts/**` and `docs/**`. **THE FINDING THAT MATTERS MOST: §5 DEFINES NO REFUSAL RECORD AT ALL.** Asked what a refusal record must contain and how it is authenticated, the agent went to the specification and found nothing — the string `refus` occurred in the whole 71 KB document only in an incidental D-028 clause. **D-012 requires that "a refusal must leave a recorded artifact, or 'the signer refused' and 'the signer was never asked' are indistinguishable" — and that requirement lives only in `decisions.md`, which implementers and labellers are denied by protocol.** So an independent implementer building from the published spec cannot implement refusal handling, which is exactly the gap D-010 was promoted into v1 to surface. Per instruction the verifier now REFUSES TO CERTIFY rather than inventing an envelope: `refused: true`, an absent receipt, or a receipt body with no signature is a hard FAIL naming what is missing, and where the bundle records a verdict the failure prints it. **Verified independently: the forged-refusal bundle that returned `=> PASS` exit 0 this morning now exits 1**, and all six genuine samples still exit 0. **A STRUCTURAL CAUSE WORTH MORE THAN THE INSTANCE: `SKIP` counted as `ok=True` in the top-level aggregate, so "was not checked" summed as "passed".** That is the shape of every gate-report defect this project has found. **Chain and vault binding:** four checks added ahead of the hash recomputations — two establishable from the bundle alone (all §5.1–§5.3 payloads must agree on `chainId`, and on `vault`), two tying the presented domain back to the signed payloads. The reasoning is sharper than the requirement: the domain separator binds the chain only against an attacker who cannot sign, and **the corpus signer key is published**, so a third-party verifier can never assume otherwise. Verified: the cross-domain bundle now fails on "every payload's chainId equals the presented domain's chainId". **Lone surrogates:** RFC 8785 §3.2.2.2 settles it — such data "MUST cause a compliant JCS implementation to terminate with an appropriate error" — so the crash was right and the error type was wrong; now a `CanonicalizationError` citing the rule, for values and keys, with valid astral pairs pinned by a control test. **Override low-s:** the spec mandates no signature encoding for either signature, so it could not settle the asymmetry; EIP-2 and §5.8's identical construction did. Stated precisely, `(r, n−s, v^1)` recovers the SAME address, so this was never forgery or replay — it was a second byte-distinct document the verifier called the same valid override. **Lossy parsing:** six byte-distinct `mandate.json` documents produced one `mandateHash`, and `bool` mapped every unrecognised string to false, so `"True"` and `"yes"` encoded as FALSE on `recurringAllowed`.
